Batch Execute/Regression Batch Execute/Regression Save Page to .PDF Batch Execute/Regression is a means of testing a ManuScript with multiple sets of data at the same time. This is useful for both development and regression testing. You can use multiple data files to execute a ManuScript by creating a batch script. In ManuScripts with multiple root nodes, Batch Execute will calculate fields outside of the primary node if they are part of the specified field's value calculation. Batch Execute results for fields outside this field's value calculation will be zero. To create and run a batch script you must define settings in the following areas: Test Name — Choose a server, user name, password, script file name, and the folder to save in. Data Files — Choose a method of acquiring data files. Report Fields — Choose a method of reporting the batch execution, and specify the field to execute. Test Results — Choose a method of viewing the batch testing results. Defining the Test Name Settings To define the Test Name settings: On the Tools menu, click Batch Execute/Regression . The Batch Execute/Regression dialog box appears. In the Server list, click the appropriate Server. If you click a Server other than (work locally) , the User name and Password text boxes appear. In the User name text box, enter your User name. In the Password text box, enter your Password. To the right of the Script File text box, click Save . The Save As dialog box appears. To navigate to and test an existing data file, click Open instead of Save . In the File name text box, enter a name for the batch script. In the Save in text box, enter the appropriate location, and then click Save . A dialog box appears. In the dialog box, click OK . Defining the Data Files Settings To define the Data Files settings: In the Data Files area, click and define a method of acquiring data files: All files in path/directory — Click the Browse button to browse for the folder that contains the data files to use. Database query/view — Use database information for the execution data. If you click this method, two text boxes appear: Use the Connection text box to specify the DSN connection of the database to use. Use the Query/View text box to specify the query to use to extract the desired data. The query must return data from two columns in the specified table or view: the QuoteID column and the XMLData column. Specified files — Select specific files for the test. Defining the Report Fields Settings To define the Report Fields settings: In the Report Fields area, click and define a method of execution reporting: Write result data files to path — Click the Browse button to select or create a file in which to contain the execution results. Write result data back to database — Write the execution results to the specified database. To use this option you must define Connection , which is the DSN name of the database to use, and Query/View , which is either the text of the query or name of the view that defines the data to use. Specified fields — Return test results for only the field or fields specified in this list. (Optional) Click the Ellipsis button to select the field to execute. If no field is specified, the tool executes the Primary Value field specified in the ManuScript properties. Defining the Test Results Settings To define the Test Results settings: In the Test Results area, click a method of viewing the results: Output to screen — View test results on screen. Output to file — Write test results to file. Click the Browse button to select or create an appropriate file or directory. If you click this method, the Output format list appears. Click the file format for the test results. Testing and Viewing Batch Scripts To test and view the results of the current batch script: Click Start . When the script is finished running, the Confirm dialog box appears. In the Confirm dialog box, click Yes to export the results or No to cancel. You can also: In the Test Results area, select the Output to screen or Output to file option, and then click View Results to see the results of the execution.

Automatic Update Automatic Update Save Page to .PDF Automatic Update option saves changes automatically, rather than asking for confirmation of each change. To use the Automatic Update feature: Click the Automatic Update button . You can also: On the Tools menu, click Automatic Update. The feature is turned on when the button appears highlighted. EXAMPLE Author ® remembers your Automatic Update settings upon exiting the application. For example, if you enable the feature, exit the application, and then open the application at a later time, the Automatic Update feature will still be enabled.

Library Wizard Library Wizard Save Page to .PDF The Library Wizard in EXAMPLE Author ® allows users to automate the creation and modification of insurance products. Using scripts created by the Script Editor Utility, you can create or modify: Premiums and coverages Underwriting rules Table data User interface Forms and print jobs The Library Wizard only appears on the Tools Menu when the Script Editor.exe file is located in the same directory as the Author.exe file. Using the Library Wizard, you can execute scripts created with the Script Editor utility in EXAMPLE Author ® . To execute a script in EXAMPLE Author ® : Open an existing ManuScript or create a new ManuScript. If you're creating a new ManuScript, you must enter catalog information. On the Tools menu, click Library Wizard . The Library Wizard appears. In the Active Catalog dropdown list, click the desired active catalog. In the Script Library tree view, click the script you wish to run. Click Process . The Wizard Steps dialog box appears. Answer the required wizard questions, and then click Finish . You can also: • Click Technical Specification to view the parameters, groups, fields, and tables to be modified, as       well as any manual steps required after the completion of the wizard. • Click Preview Requests to view the original XSLT, the param block (with answers), and the                    transformed XSLT that will be created when the user clicks Finish . Refreshing the Script Library You can refresh the Script Library in the Library Wizard at any time to ensure that: All scripts that have been added to the Script Library display in the Library Wizard. All scripts that have been removed from the Script Library will no longer display in the Library Wizard. All scripts in the Script Library are the latest versions of those scripts. To refresh the Script Library: At the bottom of the Library Wizard in EXAMPLE Author ® , click Refresh . The Script Library is updated with the latest scripts. Searching for a Script Using the Library Wizard, you can search the Script Library for a specific script file. To search for a script file: At the bottom of the Library Wizard in EXAMPLE Author ® , click Search . The Wizard Search dialog box appears. In the Search for: text box, enter the name of the script file you wish to find. You can also select the Search in Description check box to include script descriptions in the search. Click Find Next until the script file you wish to find is highlighted in the Script Library on the left. Viewing ManuScript Changes The Library Wizard allows you to view the changes to be made to the ManuScript once all wizard questions have been answered. Using this tool, you can view information about: Groups added Fields added Tables added Items that need to be modified To do items Unknown items To view changes to a ManuScript: In the LIbrary Wizard, complete all the wizard steps for a given script file. Do not click Finish . At the bottom-left corner of the Wizard Steps dialog box, click Technical Specification . The Author Wizard Information dialog box appears and displays all ManuScript changes to be made during or after script completion. Previewing Script File Requests You can preview a list of all the requests that will be used to accomplish the wizard steps for a specific script file. To preview script file requests: In the Library Wizard, complete all the wizards steps for a given script file. Do not click Finish . At the bottom of the Wizard Steps dialog box, click Preview Requests . The Author Wizard Information dialog box appears and displays all XSLT, parameters, and requests that will be used during script completion. Making Batch ManuScript Changes With the Library Wizard, you can use any script file to make multiple changes to your insurance products at any time. To use the batch functionality in the Library Wizard: When running a script in the Library Wizard in EXAMPLE Author ® , click Batch . The Batch Entry dialog box appears. In the Value column, enter the values you wish to use for the wizard step. You can also right-click in the grid and select Paste Excel Cells Into Grid to paste copied cells to the Batch Entry dialog box. All wizard steps containing batch entries must include the same number of entries. For example, if Step 1 contains three batch entries and you wish to add batch entries to Step 4, you must also add three batch entries to Step 4. Click OK . When all wizard questions have been answered, click Finish . The script runs once for each answer you provided in the Batch Entry dialog box.

Inheritance Inheritance Save Section to .PDF Save Page to .PDF The EXAMPLE Platform ® uses an inheritance model to develop and deliver ManuScripts. With the inheritance model, you can use any ManuScript as a base to create a new ManuScript. This new ManuScript, derived from the base, can extend or modify any part of the inherited ManuScript. For any ManuScript, you can specify an external ManuScript to inherit for each of the following: Fields and Groups Pages Forms For information about the Duck Creek Templates™ inheritance model, see Duck Creek Templates™ Inheritance 6.0.0.0 . Section Contents How Inheritance Works Benefits of Inheritance Issues with Inheritance Inheritance and EXAMPLE Author® Inheritance and EXAMPLE Server®

How Inheritance Works How Inheritance Works Save Page to .PDF To understand how inheritance works in the EXAMPLE Platform ® , users must understand several concepts, including the programming concept behind inheritance, key inheritance definitions, the general EXAMPLE ManuScript™ inheritance model, and the implementation of inheritance within the EXAMPLE Platform ® . The Inheritance Programming Concept The EXAMPLE Platform ® inheritance model incorporates a number of principles from the Object-Oriented Programming (OOP) technologies used in computer programming. A few important OOP definitions for words used in this document appear below. These definitions are generic in nature and reference classes or objects from a computer programming point of view. These principles apply, however, to ManuScript inheritance. Term Definition Inheritance Inheritance allows a class to extend or tailor behavior derived from a similar class to respond appropriately to specific needs. In object-oriented programming, inheritance provides a way to form new classes using pre-defined classes. These new classes use the attributes and characteristics of old classes, and then modify or extend those attributes to accommodate needs of the new class. Inheritance helps to effectively reuse existing code with a minimal number of changes. Base A base class is a class from which other classes are created or derived. Derived A derived class is a class derived from a previously defined base class. A derived class inherits all of the variables and methods of the base class from which it inherits. It may modify (or override) the behavior of any of those members or methods, and it may add new members or methods. A derived class may become the base class for another derived class. Override A derived class may replace the implementation of an aspect—typically a behavior or method—that it has inherited from its base with its own definition. When an item is redefined in this way, the item is said to have an override definition and will take on the characteristics of the new definition. Use The word use defines a relationship between two classes that are defined separately but contain objects that use (or call) methods from the other class. Two classes that use one another do not have to be based on the same class definition. Is The word is defines a relationship between a derived class and its base class. For example, when class B derives itself from class A, class B is a type of class A. Contains The contains relationship is a variation of the use relationship. A class may contain the class it uses. In this case, the creation of the referenced class is the responsibility of the calling class. EXAMPLE ManuScript™ Inheritance Definitions In addition to the previously mentioned OOP definitions, definitions of specific terms used for ManuScript inheritance can help facilitate understanding of ManuScript inheritance. The following terms are commonly used in relation to ManuScript inheritance. Term Definition Physical or Local Model Actual XML contained in the active ManuScript. This XML does not include XML from inherited ManuScripts. Logical or Assembled Model View of a derived ManuScript with all inheritance resolved and assembled. This XML image is the sum of all base ManuScripts used by the active ManuScript. For example, if B inherits A, and C inherits B, the logical model of C is the assembled view of all three ManuScripts A, B, and C. The system supports any number of inheritance layers. Local Definition Item in a ManuScript that originates in that ManuScript. A locally defined item originates and contains its definition in the active or current ManuScript. Base Definition Item in a logical model that is derived from one of the base ManuScripts and is not part of the physical model of the active ManuScript. Override Definition Item inherited from a base ManuScript that has been redefined (overridden) in the active (derived) ManuScript. An override definition is a local definition that replaces the base definition. Abstract Item Inherited item in a ManuScript that exists in the logical model only to preserve the ManuScript hierarchy. Abstract items do not override base definitions. Delete Delete an item from the base ManuScript. Demote or Revert Demote an overridden item or revert it back to its original base behavior rather than deleting it. Supersede Override entire sections of a ManuScript. This replaces base-defined fields and groups, PageSets, or forms with fields and groups, PageSets, or forms from an entirely different base ManuScript. ManuScript Inheritance Model Overview Based on OOP definitions, ManuScript inheritance can be described as allowing any ManuScript to be the base class from which a new ManuScript is derived. Any named item in the base ManuScript can have an override definition in the derived ManuScript that establishes new behaviors while inheriting all the rules and behaviors of all other items in the base ManuScript. The following diagram provides an example of the field list in a small ManuScript (ManuScript A) and some of the value definitions in this ManuScript. ManuScript A Sample of Value Definitions Data + Data.Total + Risk ¦ +Risk.Factor ¦ +Risk.Total + Coverage1 ¦ +Coverage1.BaseRate ¦ +Coverage1.Limit ¦ +Coverage1.Premium + Coverage2 ¦ +Coverage2.BaseRate ¦ +Coverage2.Limit ¦ +Coverage2.Premium Lookup from table(RateFactors) =Coverage1.Premium + Coverage2.Premium =Coverage1.BaseRate * Risk.Factor =Coverage2.BaseRate * Risk.Factor The next diagram shows the logical (or assembled) view of a second ManuScript (ManuScript B) that is derived from ManuScript A. In ManuScript B, the fields Risk.Factor and Coverage2.Premium have been modified, or given an override definition. A new field, Coverage2.Fee , has been locally defined (created) in ManuScript B and is used in the calculation of Coverage2.Premium . Logical View of ManuScript B Sample of Value Definitions Data + Data.Total + Risk ¦ +Risk.Factor ¦ +Risk.Total + Coverage1 ¦ +Coverage1.BaseRate ¦ +Coverage1.Limit ¦ +Coverage1.Premium + Coverage2 ¦ +Coverage2.BaseRate ¦ +Coverage2.Limit ¦ +Coverage2.Premium ¦ +Coverage2.Fee If condition then 1.0 else 1.25 =Coverage1.Premium + Coverage2.Premium =Coverage1.BaseRate * Risk.Factor =Coverage2.BaseRate * Risk.Factor + Coverage2.Fee =3.00 In the above examples, ManuScript A is the base ManuScript, and ManuScript B is a derived ManuScript that inherits from ManuScript A. The following observations can be made about ManuScript B: Risk.Factor — Overrides the base definition and replaces it with a new condition. Coverage2.Premium — Overrides the base definition and replaces it with a modified calculation. Coverage2.Fee — Is locally defined in ManuScript B and used by the override definition of Coverage2.Premium . From these observations, a few definitions specific to ManuScript inheritance emerge. ManuScript A can be considered a traditional ManuScript, where all information needed to operate is contained in the ManuScript itself. The actual XML stored for the ManuScript contains all the information needed for this ManuScript. This means the physical model, or the actual XML, contains all information seen and used in the ManuScript. Because it is derived from ManuScript A, ManuScript B need only keep track of the changes and additions to the base (ManuScript A). The actual XML stored for the derived ManuScript need only be those items with modifications. The logical view of ManuScript B, however, includes all the base definitions (from both ManuScripts) and the override definitions (in ManuScript B). The following diagram depicts the actual information stored (physical view) for ManuScript B. In essence, this information only includes the three items modified from the base (ManuScript A). Some additional items appear in the hierarchy below, however, because they must be present to preserve the hierarchy containing the overridden items. These items are considered abstract items, meaning they exist only for structural reasons and not for override reasons. Physical view of ManuScript B Sample of Value Definitions Data (abstract) + Risk (abstract) ¦ +Risk.Factor + Coverage2 (abstract) ¦ +Coverage2.Premium ¦ +Coverage2.Fee If condition then 1.0 else 1.25 =Coverage2.BaseRate * Risk.Factor + Coverage2.Fee =3.00 The logical view of ManuScript B defines how processing of ManuScript B occurs. The physical view defines how ManuScript B looks locally, containing only the changes made from the base ManuScript (ManuScript A). Implementation of EXAMPLE ManuScript™ Inheritance EXAMPLE ManuScripts™ indicate inheritance by an attribute on the <properties> tag at the beginning of the ManuScript XML. When a ManuScript is inherited, the inherited attribute exists and contains the ManuScriptID of the base ManuScript. This attribute is the only indicator needed to move a ManuScript into the inheritance model. <ManuScript>
  <properties manuscriptID="uniqueID" inherited="baseManuScriptID" … A ManuScript may inherit from a ManuScript that in turn inherits from another ManuScript. The EXAMPLE Platform ® supports any number of parent and child inheritance layers. Each ManuScript, however, may directly inherit from only three base ManuScripts: one for field, group, and table data; one for pages data; and one for forms data. By default, EXAMPLE Author ® displays the logical model of a ManuScript. You can change this setting to use the physical model, however, by specifying to do so in the parameters of an EXAMPLE Author ® request. EXAMPLE Server ® executes a derived ManuScript differently than it executes a traditional ManuScript. The runtime methods of the EXAMPLE Server ® (such as the <ManuScript.getValueRq> for rating and rules and the <ManuScript.getPageRq> for interview processes) operate differently between derived and traditional ManuScripts. When a user initiates an execution method for a derived ManuScript, the engine uses the logical image of the ManuScript as the runtime image when it initializes itself for runtime operations. The EXAMPLE Server ® compiles this ManuScript XML when the first request for the ManuScript is processed. This process creates the runtime instance of the assembled ManuScript rules as defined by the logical image of the ManuScript. For an inherited ManuScript to operate correctly, referenced base ManuScripts must exist on the same EXAMPLE Server ® as the derived ManuScript. Thus, because inheritance may extend through any number of layers, all the ManuScripts in a given inheritance chain must exist on a given EXAMPLE Server ® for the derived ManuScript to operate correctly. Granularity of Inheritance Levels of inheritance granularity define the points at which ManuScript overrides occur. Once an item is overridden, the override definition is responsible for the full implementation of that item. This means that when a field is overridden, all details of that field are copied into the derived ManuScript, and only the override modifications apply. That is, the modified definition replaces the inherited base definition. The following table identifies the granularity of an override—that is, the smallest item copied into the local XML of a ManuScript—when an item is overridden in a derived ManuScript. Area Overridden Item Item Copied to Local XML Details Fields View Group Group properties Overriding a group does not override child fields or sub-groups. Overriding a group copies the group to the local XML and allows editing. Field Field details Overriding a field copies the entire field to the local XML and allows editing. Tables View Table Entire table Overriding a table copies the entire table to the local XML and allows editing. Page Designer All PageSets/Pages Entire Page Designer section of a ManuScript Inheriting the Page Design section of a ManuScript from another ManuScript does not change the local source XML in the derived ManuScript. Only added or changed items are modified in the local ManuScript. When changes are made in the derived ManuScript, these individual changes are written to the local XML. PageSet PageSet properties Overriding a PageSet does not override the pages. You can modify and store the properties of the PageSet in the local XML. Page Entire page Overriding a page copies the entire page to the local XML and allows editing. Forms Mapper All forms and print job information Entire Forms Mapper section of a ManuScript Inheriting the forms section of a ManuScript from another ManuScript does not change the local source XML in the derived ManuScript. Only added or changed items are modified in the local ManuScript. When changes are made in the derived ManuScript, these individual changes are written to the local XML. Form Entire form Overriding a form copies the entire form to the local XML and allows editing. Print job Entire print job Overriding a print job copies the entire print job to the local XML and allows editing. Constant Viewer Field Entire field details Overriding a constant field in the Constant Viewer copies the entire field to the local XML and allows editing. Annotations Field Annotation only Overriding an annotation copies the annotation for a field to the local XML and allows editing. Database Mapping Mapping Entire mapping Overriding a database mapping copies the entire mapping for the ManuScript to the local XML and allows editing. Context Details Context Entire set of contexts Overriding a context copies the entire set of contexts to the local XML and allows editing.

Benefits of Inheritance Benefits of Inheritance Save Page to .PDF Inheritance brings many benefits to the EXAMPLE Platform ® , but the primary benefit is reusability. Once a ManuScript is developed and tested, you can inherit that ManuScript and apply only the changes necessary to extend or customize its logic. The following sections summarize the benefits gained by using ManuScript inheritance. ManuScript Reuse The content of a base ManuScript is automatically available to any derived ManuScript. This reuse of content applies to the initial creation of a ManuScript, but it also applies to alterations (or bug fixes) to the base ManuScript. All changes made to a base ManuScript are immediately propagated to derived ManuScripts when the base ManuScript is deployed as long as the updated fields have not been overridden in the derived ManuScripts. Simple Adoption of Upgrades and New Versions When an EXAMPLE ManuScript™ is derived from a base ManuScript, the derived ManuScript must be able to accommodate the frequent release of new base ManuScript versions. With inheritance, the adoption of new base versions is very simple. To adopt the new version of a base ManuScript into a derived ManuScript, the user can simply make a new version of the derived ManuScript and change the inherited ManuScript association to the new version of the base ManuScript. The new features and behavior of the revised base will then be instantly available to the derived ManuScript. The system preserves override features created previously in the derived ManuScript. Use, Is, Contains Relationships Prior to inheritance, only two options for using previously built items existed: importing and external reference. External reference uses a use relationship, where the items in external ManuScripts execute at runtime. Importing uses a contains relationship, bringing items from an outside ManuScript into the active ManuScript. With inheritance, however, the is relationship can now be used. A derived ManuScript is its base ManuScript. Inheritance also brings additional power to the contains relationship: once logic exists in a base ManuScript, it is immediately usable in all derived ManuScripts with only one source code to maintain. Separation of Purpose In the inheritance model, you can set up base behaviors and inherit separate base ManuScripts for specific purposes. For example, you can inherit a base ManuScript to define the fields, rating, and rules around a product. You can then inherit separate base ManuScripts for page design information and forms work. Both of these specific-purpose ManuScripts can be managed separately, yet both share the rules and definitions inherited from the main data, rates, and rules ManuScript.

Issues with Inheritance Issues with Inheritance Save Page to .PDF Although inheritance significantly enhances the EXAMPLE Platform ® , it does raise some design considerations and maintenance issues. The following sections identify some of the potential problems that can result from the inheritance model. Override Items and Base Changes Any inherited items that have been overridden in a derived ManuScript do not change when you update the base ManuScript. You must change these items manually. To do this, first identify the items that must be changed. Then demote any overridden items in the derived ManuScript to bring in the new base definitions. Finally, modify these items again in the local ManuScript to reflect the original changes that were made to the derived ManuScript. Override Items and Rating When you override an item that is used in calculations in the base ManuScript, changes you make can cause the calculations in the derived ManuScript to function incorrectly or not at all. To ensure correct rating, an overridden item can only contain changes that fit successfully into inherited rating calculations. You must also be sure to preserve any base rules and definitions not modified by the override. Separation of Purpose and Runtime Memory When derived ManuScripts inherit multiple ManuScripts for specific purposes (one for rating and rules, one for pages, and one for forms), the runtime image for each ManuScript also contains each object on which it is dependent. If the bulk of the ManuScript size is in the base ManuScript, then the logical images of the rating ManuScript, the pages ManuScript, and the forms ManuScript each consume considerable memory for the base ManuScript objects. This memory use is not an overwhelming issue, because runtime compilation of ManuScripts is machine-specific rather than user-specific. This issue, however, should still be a consideration. Renaming or Moving Base Items Because inheritance is based on the names of ManuScript items, renaming or moving items in a base ManuScript can be problematic. When you edit a base ManuScript, EXAMPLE Author ® considers items in that ManuScript to be locally defined. As a result, you can rename or move items. When you move or rename a field in a base ManuScript, however, any derived fields continue to look for the old path and field name. Furthermore, if the renamed or moved item is overridden in the derived ManuScript, the override link is broken. While the ManuScript validation process identifies these unlinked override items, this issue should be a consideration when renaming or moving items in a base ManuScript. Renaming keys in an inherited table is not a supported practice. Because table keys are used to define the structure of a table so that the ManuScript can find the appropriate data, a new table should be created when different key names are necessary.

Inheritance and EXAMPLE Author® Inheritance and EXAMPLE Author® Save Page to .PDF The display and behavior of various views in EXAMPLE Author ® support the editing of inherited ManuScripts. These views display the logical listing of items in an active ManuScript. When a user modifies a base-defined item in any of the views, that item becomes an override item. If the override item does not exist in the derived ManuScript, the system creates it, places it appropriately in the ManuScript's hierarchy, and marks it as an override entry in the ManuScript XML. The following sections describe more specifically how EXAMPLE Author ® facilitates ManuScript inheritance. Creating a Derived ManuScript When you create a new derived ManuScript, you can select the base ManuScripts from which you wish to inherit in EXAMPLE Author ® . You can select these base ManuScripts on the Inheritance tab of the ManuScript Properties dialog box ( File > Properties ). You may select a base ManuScript for field, group, and table data as well as base ManuScripts for pages and forms data, respectively. That is, in addition to inheriting a base ManuScript, you can override (or supersede) an entire section of the inherited base ManuScript with the Page Design or Forms Mapper sections of separate base ManuScripts. When selecting a base ManuScript, the root group in the active (derived) ManuScript must be identical to the root group in the base ManuScript. Viewing a ManuScript Inheritance Chain You can view a ManuScript's inheritance chain in EXAMPLE Author ® in two ways: On the Inheritance tab of the ManuScript Properties dialog box ( File > Properties ). In the Inheritance dialog box ( View > Inheritance ). ManuScript Properties Dialog Box The Inheritance tab on the ManuScript Properties dialog box ( File > Properties ) allows users to view and define a ManuScript's inheritance chain. Depending upon the type of inheritance used by a ManuScript, the Inheritance tab may display that ManuScript's inheritance chain in two ways. The Inherited ID , Inherited Page ID , and Inherited Form ID fields specify the ManuScripts directly inherited by the derived ManuScript. If a ManuScript's inheritance chain is defined using these fields, that ManuScript only contains information about ManuScripts that are directly inherited. If those inherited ManuScripts are, in turn, derived from other ManuScripts, users must open the inherited ManuScripts to view their inherited ManuScripts. Users must continue opening each ManuScript specified on the Inheritance tab of derived ManuScripts until they reach the bottom of the inheritance chain. The Inheritance Maps section specifies the entire inheritance chain for the active ManuScript. If a ManuScript's inheritance chain is defined using this section, that ManuScript contains information about each ManuScript in its inheritance chain. As a result, users can view the entire chain for each type of inheritance (data, pages, and forms) in this section without opening any additional ManuScripts. Inheritance Dialog Box The Inheritance dialog box ( View > Inheritance ) allows users to view an entire ManuScript inheritance chain for each type of inheritance (data, pages, and forms) and, if desired, open any ManuScript in that chain. Visual Configuration Options In EXAMPLE Author ® , you can use the Options settings to define font name, size, and style of items that can have their attributes edited in EXAMPLE Author ® . You can define these items to visually highlight their scope in EXAMPLE Author ® . Note the Display options for lists and inherited indicators area of the dialog box below. Demote/Revert Inherited items that have been overridden in EXAMPLE Author ® can return to the base definition for those items by being demoted/reverted . The demote/revert feature of EXAMPLE Author ® follows the granularity rules outlined in Granularity of Inheritance . Inheritance Rules Because various items that appear in a derived ManuScript do not always reside in that ManuScript, rules govern the actions you can take on these items. You cannot, for example, rename or move items in a derived ManuScript that reside in the base ManuScript. The following table defines the actions available to users working in a derived ManuScript. Action/Activity Action Available in Active ManuScript Base-Defined Items Overridden Items Locally Defined Items Modify/Edit Overrides the item. Modifies the item. Allowed Add n/a n/a Allowed Duplicate Creates the duplicate as a locally defined item. Creates the duplicate as a locally defined item. Allowed Delete Denied Demotes/reverts the item back to the base definition. Deletes the local override definition. Allowed Rename Denied Denied Allowed Move (Fields) Denied Denied Allowed Reorder (Pages/Forms) Allowed. This action creates an abstract local copy that specifies the order in the active ManuScript. Allowed Allowed View XML You can view any of three XML displays for an inherited ManuScript in EXAMPLE Author ® : Source XML, Logical XML, and Compare XML. Source XML displays the local XML where modifications to the base ManuScript must be made. Logical XML displays the assembled view of the derived ManuScript XML and all sections of base ManuScripts used by the derived ManuScript. Compare XML compares the Logical XML of the derived ManuScript to the Logical XML of the base ManuScript. Import Restrictions When importing ManuScripts in EXAMPLE Author ® , ManuScripts already inherited by the active ManuScript are excluded from the ManuScript Catalog. This prevents cyclical use of the same material in a single ManuScript. ManuScript Validation and Inheritance The ManuScript validation process in EXAMPLE Author ® identifies any orphaned override items. During validation, if the active ManuScript contains an item marked as an override, but the item name does not exist in any of the base ManuScripts, an error occurs. The user cannot rename or re-associate the override item with the renamed item in the base. If no associated item exists in the base, the item can be converted to a local definition or deleted. The validator cannot check to see if an orphaned item is used by an external ManuScript. During the validation process, however, you can instruct the validator to skip a given item in future validations. This function is useful for items detected as orphaned that are used by external ManuScripts. XML Attributes The Page Designer and Forms Mapper tools in EXAMPLE Author ® create the XML attribute prevPage , which facilitates the ordering of items in a derived ManuScript. Because a derived ManuScript only contains additional or overridden pages in its local XML, it must use the prevPage attribute to correctly order additional and overridden pages with the inherited pages. The prevPage attribute identifies which inherited page the new or overridden page should follow. Thus, this attribute controls where to insert a page in the list of pages.

Inheritance and EXAMPLE Server® Inheritance and EXAMPLE Server® Save Page to .PDF The following sections describe how EXAMPLE Server ® facilitates ManuScript inheritance. Deployment Deployment sends the physical image of all ManuScripts needed by a given ManuScript to the target EXAMPLE Server ® . The deployment process includes all ManuScripts upon which the active ManuScript is dependent. Runtime Loading of Logical Model When the runtime version of a ManuScript is needed, the logical view of the ManuScript is assembled on the first demand for the ManuScript. As a result, all ManuScripts upon which the active ManuScript is dependent must exist on the EXAMPLE Server ® . Development Platform Modifications When working on a development EXAMPLE Server ® used by multiple developers, users need to ensure that runtime images loaded for rating and testing are correct each time a runtime request is initiated. Because the EXAMPLE Server ® maintains a cache of the ManuScripts in memory, runtime components built from the logical image in memory may be stale. Changes recently made to the base ManuScripts may not appear in the cached logical image. To accommodate this problem, you can configure an EXAMPLE Server ® to be a development server. This configuration forces the runtime image of a ManuScript to refresh each time a user initiates a request. This development configuration, however, comes with a performance cost and should be reserved for development servers only.

Viewing and Editing XML Viewing and Editing XML Save Page to .PDF You can update ManuScripts in XML with the XML Viewer dialog box. You can view XML for individual fields or tables, or for the entire ManuScript. Use the buttons on the left hand side of the XML Viewer to expand or collapse the XML view. Viewing XML for a Field To view XML for a field: Click the field. On the Edit menu, click View Field XML . The XML Viewer dialog box appears for the selected field. In this dialog box, you can: Find an item within the XML Edit and update the XML Viewing XML for a Table To view XML for a table: Click the table. On the Edit menu, click View Table XML . The XML Viewer dialog box appears for the selected table. In this dialog box, you can: Find an item within the XML Edit and update the XML Viewing XML for a Page To view XML for a page: Right-click the page, group, layout, or field. Click View Page XML . The XML Viewer dialog box appears for the selected page or for the page containing the selected group, layout, or field. In this dialog box, you can: Find an item within the XML Edit and update the XML Viewing XML for a ManuScript To view XML for a ManuScript: On the View menu, point to XML , and then click Source XML . The XML Viewer dialog box appears for the ManuScript. In this dialog box, you can: Find an item within the XML Edit and update the XML You can also view logical XML or differences XML. Viewing XML Differences To view XML differences for an inherited ManuScript: On the View menu, point to XML , and then click Differences XML . The XML Comparison dialog box appears for the ManuScript. In this dialog box, you can view the differences between the current ManuScript and the base ManuScript. Editing XML To edit XML: Open the XML Viewer dialog box for a field, table, or ManuScript. Use the following functions to edit the XML, and then click Update : Function Mouse Keyboard Un-Indent BACKSPACE Delete marked text DELETE End of line END Create new line ENTER Beginning of line HOME Change insert or overwrite mode INSERT Bottom of document CTRL+END Top of document CTRL+HOME Copy marked text CTRL+INSERT Undo editing CTRL+Z Move to next word CTRL+ right or left arrow key Cut marked text SHIFT+DELETE Paste from clipboard SHIFT+INSERT Mark text Drag SHIFT+PAGE, SHIFT+END, or SHIFT+arrow keys

Setting Field Descriptors Setting Field Descriptors Save Page to .PDF Fields View The EXAMPLE Platform ® contains a Descriptors feature that enables ManuScript developers to flag fields for specific purposes. Fields can be assigned any descriptor and are searchable by their assigned descriptor. Out of the box, the EXAMPLE Platform ® recognizes the following descriptors. The following descriptors are available for preliminary implementation with the 8/10/2011 release of EXAMPLE Platform ® 4.3. However, the full rating functionality required to use these descriptors during audit processing is  targeted for a subsequent release. Descriptor Types and Purposes Type Description Audit:Indicator Identifies whether a specific coverage is auditable. This descriptor should be applied to an integer field that has the values of 0 , 1 , or 2 , and can be used in conjunction with the Auditable indicator on WrittenPrem fields to enable exposures to be auditable only during specific scenarios. A field labeled with the Audit:Indicator descriptor needs to reside under the same public group as the related field labeled with the Audit:Exposure descriptor. The two fields can reside in separate private groups as long as they share a parent GUID. The options are: 0 — Non-auditable 1 — Auditable Exposure (such as a monetary amount) 2 — Auditable Units (such as the number of employees) Because a boolean only returns a value of 0 or 1 , you must use an integer field. Audit:Segment Identifies a field that should cause an audit segment to occur. A change to fields marked with this descriptor during a transaction (such as an endorsement) will flag the transaction as one that causes a new segment to occur. Audit:Exposure Identifies auditable exposure fields. This descriptor is used by EXAMPLE Server ® to determine which values need to be collected for each segment during an audit transaction. A field labeled with the Audit:Exposure descriptor needs to reside under the same public group as the related field labeled with the Audit:Indicator descriptor. The two fields can reside in separate private groups as long as they share a parent GUID. They must live under the same public group. Audit: A custom value, defined by the ManuScript developer, used to identify additional information to be used during audit processing. EXAMPLE Server ® does not use this data out of the box. It can be used, however, in custom ManuScript logic. For example, it could be used to identify the fields that capture original exposure estimates for display during an audit transaction. To define a custom audit value: Enter Audit: , and then enter a caption for the custom descriptor. For example, Audit:Estimate . The caption entered for the custom descriptor must be a valid XML name.

Appendix Appendix This section includes examples of XML for importing Excel files into EXAMPLE Author ® by mapping XML.

XML Examples XML Examples Save Page to .PDF Creating an Upload public group <object id="Upload" path="upload">
   <minOccurs value="1" />
   <maxOccurs value="1" />
</object> Creating a UploadInput private group and field <object id="UploadInput" path="">
   <minOccurs value="1" />
   <maxOccurs value="1" />
</object> <public id="UploadInput.FilePath" path="FilePath" type="string">
   <definition>
      <caption value="Select the Excel file:" />
      <formatMask value="file" />
      <readOnly idref="UploadPrivate.HasFilePath" />
      <maxLength value="255" />
   </definition>
</public> Creating an Upload Private private group and field <object id="UploadPrivate" path="">
   <minOccurs value="1" />
   <maxOccurs value="1" />
</object> <private id="UploadPrivate.HasFilePath" type="boolean" path="HasFilePath">
   <value>
      <comparison compare="ne">
         <operand idref="UploadInput.FilePath" type="string" />
         <operand type="string" value="" />
      </comparison>
   </value>
</private> <private id="UploadPrivate.HasNoFilePath" type="boolean" path="HasNoFilePath">
   <value>
      <comparison compare="eq">
         <operand idref="UploadInput.FilePath" type="string" />
         <operand type="string" value="" />
      </comparison>
   </value>
</private> <private id="UploadPrivate.ResetFilePath" type="string" path="ResetFilePath">
   <value value="" />
   <affects>
      <reference idref="UploadInput.FilePath" effect="setValue" />
   </affects>
</private> Collecting Specific Columns <object id="Mapping" path="mapping">
   <minOccurs value="1" />
   <maxOccurs value="1" />
</object> Including the [@valueMap=&quot;1&quote;] in the path attribute <object id="MapType" path="Type[@valueMap=&quot;1&quot;]">
<minOccurs value="1" />
<maxOccurs value="1" />
</object> Default value and Caption values <public id="MapType.column" path="@column" type="string">
<definition>
<caption value="Type" />
<options>
<request verb="ExcelImport.getColumnNamesRq">
<responsePath value="columns/column" />
<captionPath value="@caption" />
<valuePath value="@name" />
<nameValues>
<nameValue idref="UploadInput.FilePath"
name="fileName" dataType="attribute" />
</nameValues>
</request>
</options>
<formatMask value="blankOption:Not Applicable" />
</definition>
<rules>
<value>
<request verb="ExcelImport.inferValueRq" get="@column">
<default value="" />
<nameValues>
<nameValue idref="UploadInput.FilePath"
name="fileName" dataType="attribute" />
<nameValue value="string" name="dataType"
dataType="attribute" />
</nameValues>
</request>
</value>
<default value="Type" />
</rules>
</public>
